ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e6025	High house! Ladies and gentlemen! In an interview by the President of the European Commission, Santer, in the summer of the "Small Newspaper", he says: "We are currently experiencing a crisis of confidence within the European Union. We need to restore confidence in our citizens together. Of course, Member States have their homework to do." I therefore believe that today we should also use the foreign policy debate in the direction of questioning the Austrian Federal Government: How has the homework actually been solved with regard to European integration in Austria? Is there not a number of contradictions between the two government parties that need clarification at least once? Mr. Vice-Chancellor! What about foreign policy, for example, in matters of security policy, where you can always relativize neutrality to you, on the basis of public statements, and take the view that we are moving towards WEU and NATO membership, while on the other hand, your coalition partner is acting as if neutrality were sacrosan and should be anchored once and for all in a future European security architecture as a fixed component of Austria? So what about it? Is there an official line of this federal government? What about economic policy? – On the one hand, social and environmental declarations are given by the two government parties, but on the other hand, the European Parliament is voting against the introduction of, for example, the labelling obligation of genetically modified foods. Even your own group has prevented this essential environmental measure, but also consumer protection measures, from being implemented in the European Parliament. What about the banishment of the risk of social dumping? . Today, especially on the day of the world's children, we really need to be aware that Europe is currently open to a world economy in which products are not differentiated under what conditions. Where child labour is on the agenda, where elementary social rights are disregarded, where fundamental and freedom rights are trampled with feet, where young people are working in night and shift work, it is of course much cheaper to produce products than they can be developed here under the strict social and environmental standards in Austria. These products are then imported in order to actually take away business and jobs from Austrian businesses as well. What is the answer here to the official policy of this Federal Government? – To this day, only silence! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] What about the freedom of movement? . I remember very well that Dr. Mock always said during the first negotiations that one of the essential elements of this European Union was the free movement of persons. – Yesterday, the Federal Chancellor said that this should not be so, because it is dangerous because it involves wage dumping – something that we told you before EU accession in 1994. What kind of line does the government have now? Are you in favour of combating wage dumping and thus restricting the free movement of persons, or do you continue to apply the four freedoms to which Austria has also been familiar under the Treaty with the European Union? How do we look at what economic policy path we are going? Are we going on the path of monetary rehabilitation in order to achieve the Maastricht criteria, or are we going to follow the path your socialist partner has repeatedly announced by saying that we are basically pursuing a policy of employment policy, including what European economic policy is going on? – These are two basic things. Vrannitzky is constantly announcing EU employment initiatives, and you are more closely following the path of monetary rehabilitation in order to achieve the Maastricht criteria. What way is the Austrian Federal Government really going here? Or what about the Easter enlargement? What's going on here? – On the one hand, social democrats say that this is dangerous at the moment, on the other hand, yesterday in Parliament, you said that the enlargement of the East was, so to speak, something completely elementary, which should be sought immediately. These are the things where we ask you: What about the completion of the homework by the Federal Government in order to restore a coherent foreign policy? I believe that enlargement is an example of how official foreign policy principles are increasingly difficult to reconcile with the objectives of a national economic policy. We need to note today, not least through the wrong integration policy of recent years, a significant increase in unemployment in Austria, and we must note that the EU's economic policy – opening up, contracts with potential new member states, such as the reform countries, but also opening up, for example, towards other partners with whom we are associated, for example, with Turkey – will also lead to a massive collapse on the Austrian labour market. If I only reported in the "Standard" of 26. In August of this year, it should be read that even the current EU candidate of the SPÖ, Ettl, says: "Europe needs to get involved in the textile industry because otherwise we will lose thousands of jobs." And the Austrian representative of the textile and clothing industry says: "Only by means of the customs agreement with Turkey which has been in force since the beginning of the year, 150 000 jobs are destroyed in the European textile industry." – So in an EU that already has 18 million and more unemployed, which already has to spend 4.5 trillion Shillings for unemployment support, such opening clauses, such contracts – for example with Turkey, for example with the countries of the reform through the enlargement of the East – destroy hundreds of thousands of jobs. And Mr. Ettl, your government partner, will say: That is where we must push Austria, and we must push the EU. Yes, what do you want now, ladies and gentlemen? – On the one hand, you are playing for us the big Europeans, the great world politicians who represent liberalisation, and on the other hand, when it comes to getting involved, you say that now we have to stop Austria again. – Tell us what you really do! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] They have offered more than 4 300 jobs in the textile industry alone through EU policy in recent years. In almost a year, more than 4 300 jobs in the textile and clothing industry in Austria have been lost, more than 4,000 jobs in the food industry. And now Mr. Ettl himself says: "You have to put on the brake," demands the head of the textile trade union Ettl. "We cannot continue liberalisation, otherwise jobs will be reinvigorated." I have such doubts that you are aware of what this really means for the Austrian economy. East opening sounds good, but ask your farmer in your own party, which means being under the pressure of cheap products from Eastern Europe! Ask the workers' representatives what it means to go into this eastern opening unprepared! They are making themselves up and talking about opening up to the east, but you are not saying that fears are also significant in your own government group and in your government partner that there is a further dramatic increase in unemployment and the destruction of jobs. These are the contradictions that arise daily, and that is why we liberals say again with all clarity: They did not do the homework after accession to the EU and risk thousands and thousands of additional unemployed and the destruction of jobs in Austria. And that can't be it! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] And yesterday we are told such an adventurous fairy tale again by saying that there is now a posting directive that prevents it from coming to this underage of the wage and collective contract level in Austria. ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e6238	Mr President. Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The foreign policy report, which is today the real subject of the agenda, gives an overview of foreign policy. I would therefore like to refer first to this report before I refer to the statements made by Mr Haider in the second part. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Stadler.]] This foreign policy report is not, as has been said in an interlocutory call by Mr Stadler, propaganda, but... [[Abg. May. Stadler: Populism and Propaganda! Pure propaganda!]] [[Abg. Tichy-Schreiter: He didn't read it!]] I ask you to check for yourself, ladies and gentlemen. It is a description of what has been done in the foreign policy sector by the Foreign Office, partly also by the Federal Chancellery and also by other departments. This is a good yearbook of Austrian foreign policy, which is to be maintained, which may be partially streamlined and changed – talks have already taken place about it – but which is a good presentation for every interested person and shows what happened in Austrian foreign policy last year. It was also right to include in the 1995 foreign policy report the first year in the EU and therefore also to give an account of what has happened from the Austrian side in order to evaluate our own work, especially in view of the debate on how good or not good Austria is in the EU. It is clear, ladies and gentlemen, that foreign policy, the international policy of Austria as a whole is carried out by more people and bodies than only by the Foreign Office as part of the government as a whole or by the government as a whole. The foreign policy of a state – every state! – has become very diverse. In addition to the traditional foreign representation function of the foreign office, the specific form of the EU and the Councils have increasingly given priority to the activity of the individual departments in Brussels and therefore also to contacts with other countries. This broader, more diverse foreign policy is not limited to the traditional foreign office or diplomatic part. In addition to the work of the government itself, the activities of the interest groups, which are also part of the EU's bodies, for example, the bilateral activities of the Austrian Parliament, as well as the activities of European and international organisations have also taken place. To this end, the work of the European Parliament itself, in which the Austrian Members also make a decisive contribution. Nor should we forget that non-governmental organisations have also become an essential factor in international policy – we are thinking of human rights issues, of educational work and of elections in different countries, for example, we are thinking of development policy measures. It is therefore necessary to see a country's foreign policy beyond its traditional jurisdiction, which is characterised by the distribution of resources. If, on the occasion of the foreign policy report and the yearbook, I would like to express my thanks here on behalf of the Foreign Policy Committee to the bodies and officials concerned, I would like to include a thank you from Parliament to all those who, in organizations and at the most diverse levels, strive to be active in the interests of our country abroad or in international organisations. Their work is also very important for our Republic. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] Secondly, ladies and gentlemen, if we talk so much about the issue of common foreign policy in the EU, we must be clear that this is an area in which it is important to bring in Austrian interests, but which will be more concerned with the common foreign policy in the European field than with the representation of Austrian interests and concerns. This common foreign policy will not only be an arithmetic means of the interests of the individual Member States, but we must understand that this common foreign policy also obliges us to consider: What do we want? Also to think about what the EU wants in Europe, in European progress, on a global scale, so that we do not only understand the EU as a continuation of our own wishes – that is also legitimate and important – but rather as a new level with more will and more action than just one's own. I believe that this is important in terms of content and, as a result, organisational and technical considerations which must be undertaken by each country, but also jointly for the EU. In connection with this, I would also like to take up Parliament's willingness to consider, together with the Foreign Office, but also with other bodies. Of course, Austria will now focus on the preparation of the EU Presidency. I am glad that the preparations are already under way, including at the parliamentary level, because Parliamentary events are also to be considered in this context. In the context of this and the following, the question is: What should the formulation of this common foreign policy look like, in terms of content to organisational-technical? It's important. This also makes a difference from the other Community policies of the European Union – in the economic field, for example, we have a long process of grinding, I would say, towards European integration, because we have concluded our Treaty with the European Economic Community in the early 1970s. In this area, we have therefore for two decades taken the path of integration. At the beginning of the 1990s, we even took the intermediate stage of the European Economic Area before joining the EU and hence the Single Market. So we have had a process of change in this area, and yet, as we know, it is difficult for many Austrians to find out and to see the clear lines and the benefits of a single market in the European Union. We are, of course, doing much harder in the area of security policy, because by 1989 we had no reason at all to make any changes. It was clear: We're neutral! There was the East-West conflict. Neutrality was a very functional safety and foreign policy maxim. Only after 1989 has a debate started slowly on whether all of this is still so meaningful. I would like to make this so clear because I clearly recognise that a large proportion of the population is still mentally dependent on neutrality. Why do I mean that this isn't possible? – Neutrality has no content, as sometimes the impression is given in the discussions of the government parties. Neutrality has a very clear international scope, that is to say, stand out from all conflicts, regardless of the motives from which they are led. Neutrality has something to do with equal treatment in the case of conflict. It also says quite clearly: To prevent misuse of its own territorium in the event of conflict – therefore armed neutrality. And we should not forget that Austrian neutrality has something to do with the Swiss pattern. That is why I think it is really grossly negligent when the government parties suggest that the debate on European security structures is being reversed, re-imposed, but at the same time is constantly claimed: There is the possibility of combining common security and foreign policy with permanent neutrality. This is a square of the circle that is not possible. We should admit this openly and not nebulously to the population and pretend to be both possible. That's the accusation we need to make you. [[Congratulations at the Liberal Forum.]] Ladies and gentlemen! It is therefore necessary to make it clear. I'd like to point out what we're talking about. It is an excellent objective of the European Union to bring about a peace and security policy in Europe, to strengthen it, to improve it. Since Maastricht there is at least the intention – and I would be careful – to become a European Union which is indeed capable of action in the field of foreign and security policy. It is indeed a joke that this European Union, which is economically strong, is the rich population which has many opportunities, is so weak, disputed in the foreign and security policy field and continues to act in national actions. We know from other areas, such as the economy, social policy, environmental policy, how useful it is, when this continent, at least once the European Union, acts together, however, in the field of foreign and, above all, in the field of security policy, we are beginning to become anxious and fall back into old patterns of knitting. It is said to be a militarization of the European Union. That's a misevaluation. It is clear to us that if this European Union acts in a common foreign policy and security policy, it will bring its strength to bear in the interests of an external policy aimed at extending human rights, safeguarding ethnic fundamental rights, peacekeeping and stability in this continent and its neighbouring regions. That is why we believe that this common foreign and security policy, as it stands in Maastricht as a concept, will actually be a reality. We believe that Austria should be involved in the construction of this common European security policy from the outset in order to support this progress. Conflicts in Europe – even if we are not directly threatened, we know – concern everyone. That is why, in this case too, common policy, common security measures and, as a last resort, joint military effort is needed if one is to achieve political objectives by military means, by force. In this case, Ultima ratio is considered the last instrument to be military capacity. But long before that, it is important to pursue preventive security policy. This is what the European Union is doing by trying to achieve and extend economic and social stability with an external policy that eliminates the causes of conflict. This is the essential element of a common foreign and security policy, and not how we had to experience it, that Europe in the Balkans simply had to capitulate de facto before the use of force, in the most brutal way we have experienced it, and only when the United States was willing to intervene in the military – I would say that for years, at least to hundreds of thousands of dead too late – has succeeded in curtailing, stranglering and hopefully bringing about stability there. We must recognise these things, and we should take part in the progress of Europe's united security policy, knowing that neutrality, as we have taken it as a international commitment, is not compatible with this common European security policy. This is a fact that can be proved. Whether we continue to act as an individual state in security policy or whether we are devoting ourselves to this common European foreign and security policy, we clearly decide on the common European foreign and security policy. I am convinced that, if we open and clear this discussion, the Austrian people, who have now been very clearly and repeatedly presented neutrality for decades as the central foreign and security policy instrument, therefore depends on favouring this concept of the progress of a common foreign and security policy in terms of peace stability. That's why: If we want this open discussion, let us express these objectives openly, we do not hide behind a neutrality paradise that has actually become ineffective and which we have actually pushed aside with the accession to the European Union. We should have the courage to say these things clearly, then the people will understand us and I am sure that they will also follow this path.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e7051	Mr President. Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen! I also assume that the development of free trade in the world is one of the essential foundations for economic growth. Secondly, it must be seen that the fruits of this economic growth are relatively uneven even after the last world economic report, namely that the countries of the north continue to gain disproportionately, while the countries of the south fall in total, although there are also some exceptions there that have experienced an amazing growth in recent years. What is happening everywhere, however, whether in North or South, is that a consequence of globalisation is a stronger inequality in society between poor and rich. In Europe, this is still much less the case than in the United States of America and in those parts of the world, where growth is currently the strongest, for example in China and in other South East Asian countries. Of course, the question is: How can we address negative, undesirable liberalisation consequences, and what are possible answers? This would mean that there is also a guarantee of minimum social standards in the European Union. I believe that it is foolishly important for this whole development of liberalisation to bring in certain minimum limits, because there is, of course, a danger that developments which are often overshadowed by national measures are taking place. A fourth point in this context, which is also my concern: It discussed the further development of the European Union, the opening of the East and the like. In fact, the opening of the East is a complicated issue, although our political stance is quite clear: We are in favour of the enlargement of the European Union, we are in favour of the integration of the reform states as quickly as possible. That is clear, there are political, security, economic considerations. Nevertheless, I think it is right to discuss how and how this integration runs. For I do not know whether it would be so good for all Eastern European countries to be a member tomorrow, whether their economies are already competitive to a degree that they can survive so well in the harsh wind of competition in the European Union. Whether it is also cheaper for the Eastern European countries to say the whole thing is a project for the next six or seven years – and I do not want to make any forecasts – in order to carry out this transition properly is in space. Second point: Of course, we need to clarify political and strategic questions. Everyone knows that there is only going to be enlargement with institutional reform. And institutional reform of the European Union means reform of the distribution of power: Who has a gain in power, who has a loss of power, how much sovereignty is one willing to delegate to European institutions? This is a very, very delicate matter, but a question for this Easter enlargement. The third point, which you have to clarify honestly, is: Will the enlargement of the East cause additional costs? Do these additional costs mean that subsidies to the Mediterranean countries will be reduced? Is that possible politically? Do these additional costs result in a change in the budget of the European Union and, for example, in a reduction in expenditure from major expenditure heads, such as reform of the agricultural system, or in the fact that the net payers have an increased contribution to the budget of the European Union? And what democratic acceptance will this take place in each Member State? I believe that these are the concrete questions that arise in this context of Easter enlargement and which we have to discuss and negotiate properly. For one thing is also to be prevented: that by an excess of euphoria, which goes beyond the real potential, the population is mirrored, the European train travels at 250 kilometres, although in reality it simmers very comfortably with only solid 80 or 90 kilometres. People could have the impression that they are being overwhelmed, regardless of the real pace. True is what Delors has always said: Nothing is possible against people or without people, nothing remains without the institutions. That is why we also need the democratic approval of the population for this process of enlargement. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e7799	Mr President. Mrs. State Secretary! High house! Meanwhile, the Nuclear Test Stop Treaty is also in place for ratification, and at its last meeting this week, the Austrian Council of Ministers approved this Convention on a comprehensive ban on nuclear testing. The Treaty provides for a verification mechanism, and for its central office Vienna is happily planned – Vienna in its role as the seat of the UN, but also as the seat of international organisations that can work for peace in the world. Progress has been made in a number of ways in the case of bans on conventional weapons. One example of this is the adoption of the Protocol on Laser Weapons, on Weapons that Create Blindness. Generally, there is also progress in the ban on anti-personnel mines – a long effort. A joint action by the EU for the first time provides for a total ban, an export moratorium and, most importantly, mine clearance actions. In order to support this joint action, national bans are now being encouraged, which we in Austria are not least grateful – and I would like to stress this – for the support of the Foreign Minister and Vice-Chancellor Schüssel will hopefully finally be enforced in the near future. Progress must be made, particularly in the area of mine clearance. Mines are easily laid, but hard to remove. At the beginning of July this year, a conference on mine space technology was held in Denmark, which was largely ignored by the media. There we have devoted ourselves to the necessary research, approval and more efficient design of mine space actions, because, in the face of hundreds of thousands of munitions, mine clearance actions need to be cheaper and more effective. Austria not only has the economic opportunity to engage actively in this, because in Austria we have in addition to a company producing mine space equipment, also a company that deals with the delaboration, i.e., the disintegration of these terrible weapons. The Austrian Federal Army could also develop and use its know-how in this field and become more active in this field. Peace policy and security policy have determined the foreign policy debate of this morning. Many of my previous speakers have raised questions of peace and security policy, but unfortunately only from the very limited aspect of arms, arms policy. However, real peace policy can only be disarmament policy. It is long-lasting, it is difficult, disarmament policy is characterized by countless setbacks, hope that has not been fulfilled because powerful lobbys of the arms industry, which arms exporters oppose and oppose. Nevertheless, or, speaking to Rosa Luxembourg, despite all of this, disarmament policy must continue, because only it guarantees genuine peace. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e8285	Mr President. Mrs. State Secretary! High house! The foreign policy report is the first report on our membership in the European Union, for the first year in this European Union. Let me take this report to the point of taking out two issues that are of particular interest to all Austrians, but above all to young people in this country. One is the question of environmental protection, and the second is the question of nuclear power and the nuclear threat in Europe. We have already pointed out in the negotiations with the European Union that it is one of our objectives not to have to lower the high environmental standards achieved by Austria by joining the European Union. The same applies to nuclear power. Austria has made a clear commitment, namely a clear rejection of the use of nuclear power and has also brought it into the negotiations on the European Union. During this first year in the European Union, we have also shown very clearly that we are opposed to the so-called peaceful use of nuclear energy, because it is always and still poses dangers and is not absolutely manageable by people. That was always our opinion in Austria's neighbourhood policy. Austria is surrounded by nuclear power plants, which not only do not comply with Western safety standards, but on the contrary often pose a particular danger to the Austrian population in a combination of Soviet construction and attempts at Western retrofitting. This last year, we have very vehemently opposed the completion of the Mochovce nuclear power plant, and we have succeeded in a united effort – and above all in highlighting the role of Parliamentarians in the European Parliament – that the proposed loans from the European Bank for Construction and Development have not been granted. However, it has not yet been said that the Mochovce nuclear power plant has in the meantime been depreciated or even died for our neighbours. On the contrary: There are now efforts to complete this nuclear power plant with the help of a German loan and with the help of Siemens. This means that in our external relations to our friends – and we are interested in friendly contacts with our neighbouring countries – we must clearly explain our position and the position of the Austrians: A long-term exit from nuclear energy in Europe must be achieved. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] It is precisely the events of the last few days in the Chernobyl nuclear power plant, which has found too sad fame and which has brought disease and death to thousands and thousands of thousands of people, that the danger is in no way averted. It is quite worrying and worrying that the European States, but also the G7, have not yet reached a consensus on how Chernobyl can be closed, and are not prepared to make the financial resources available for it. I believe that it must be one of our tasks, particularly in the field of nuclear power, to put in place all the initiatives within the European Union in order to enable this exit. In the same way, our efforts in the field of environmental protection are required. We have achieved in the Treaty negotiations that those standards which are higher in Austria than in the European Union must be reviewed by the Union in the context of a review process. Where appropriate, the Union shall bring its directives into line with the standards of Austria – not only Austria but also the other two candidate countries Sweden and Finland, which in many areas were equally ahead of the European Union. This review process has now begun, and it will show that Austria, Sweden and Finland will make every effort in these areas to achieve this improvement in standards. This is particularly necessary when, in view of the enlargement of the European Union, it is precisely those countries that are pushing into this European Union, which have a huge need for recovery on environmental protection issues, but also an enormous need to help the European Union improve environmental standards in its countries. It would be very important for the European Union to make appropriate resources available now to improve the environmental situation, especially in the neighbouring countries of Austria. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] However, a very essential point, ladies and gentlemen, is the inclusion of environmental protection in the Treaties of the European Union, an initiative which the Vice-Cancellor and Foreign Minister, Dr Wolfgang Schüssel, gratefully, has taken to achieve, in addition to the anchoring of environmental protection in the European Union, the anchoring of animal welfare in the Treaties of the European Union. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I believe that precisely the terrible pictures of animal transport, as we have repeatedly seen in recent months, are the painful methods by which animals are treated throughout the world and across Europe, enough to lead the way and achieve European directives for both farm animal husbandry and pet farming. The main aim is to prevent animal transport across Europe, to protect habitats of animals, to disregard cruel treatment of animals, for example to prohibit and not to promote animal torture, and to create reasonable conditions for absolutely necessary or inevitable animal transport. There are still a number of other demands that we have made in an appropriate initiative, and I am particularly pleased that the Austrian People's Party's mandates will also pursue this initiative and thus contribute to anchoring animal welfare across Europe.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e8572	We're now on the 2nd. ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e8599	Mr President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! The motion 239/A by my colleagues and my colleagues, with whom the Federal Law on the Employment of Children and Youths is amended, includes flexible working time planning during the summer time, a shift in daily working time by one hour for young people, no extension, no extension, no introduction of daily working hours, no form of calculation period, but nothing but the possibility of a shift in daily working time for young people by one hour into the evening. There are problems in the employment of young people when, though the summer time is changed, the need for a work performance depends on the astronomical period. Even if the young person wanted to do so, and if the rest period was continuous, it would not be possible, in accordance with the legislation currently in force. This leads to difficulties, as will certainly be known to all of us, especially in the hospitality sector, where the needs of holidaymakers and other guests are less aligned according to the official time, but just according to the sun. There are plenty of examples. All of us who sit a little longer in the summer know how to report it. ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e10456	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Once again, it is about the debate on a concept of security that corresponds to a world that is constantly changing. I therefore advocated several times, including from this point of view, not to reduce our understanding of security solely to the purely military aspect. Even if one considers that one should not always focus on the opinion of the people, it is objective to note that security issues concern the concerns of having a safe job in the future, in order to ensure that the pollution and pollution of the environment outside our borders should not be an additional burden on Austria. It is about the concerns about the transfer of international crime to our country. It concerns the concerns of drug trafficking, drug abuse, trafficking in human beings, and it is ultimately about the concerns of having a secure evening of life and ensuring a training place for young people. Of course, we are also concerned that war and violence, as we unfortunately have to experience in the world again and again, not in our homeland, do not cross over to Europe. That is to say, the need of the Austrians for security is not primarily determined by a military threat. The cold war, the East-West dispute is a good thing about history. And, in the meantime, we Austrians have moved into a new, common Europe, which is about the economic, cultural exchange, common research and development, about the joint design of our future. And because we have moved into this new Europe, it is now about shaping it. I believe that Austria can say - we debated this only yesterday, ladies and gentlemen - that it is successful to create meaningful jobs on a subject in this integration model, that it is successful to push forward the common fight against international crime. I am sure that we will also be successful if we stand up for a Europe where a security system is to be created that meets our needs as well. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! But it really does not need the repeated demand for neutrality. And we also do not need the kind of prayer to neutrality that is called for in some way. Austria has proved for years and decades that it has applied neutrality, that it has also used this neutrality to continue to engage in mediation functions in certain historical constellations, and that it has been successful in adapting this principle to its own security needs over and over again. Mrs Petrovic! If you have previously referred to President Chirac and his opinion here, with France's nuclear weapons potential, to pre-empt Europe's common defence architecture, I would ask you to remember that at the time when we discussed nuclear experiments in the Pacific, I strictly refused to recognise for us and for the European Union such an exclusive right of representation of the nuclear shield. I mean, in the fact that we do not want to take part in any wars in the future, that we do not want to station foreign troops in our country, that we do not want to participate in any military alliance, nothing has changed at all. I am glad to say to you that the Austrians' self-understanding of their country, their status, is so strengthened by their role in the international system that they cannot shake the artificial maintenance of a neutrality discussion or the questioning of the Austrian nation. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Now to the Intergovernmental Conference, which is just meeting. Ladies and gentlemen! The Austrian Federal Government has not rarely made it clear from this government bank that next year we will be subject to a new review of the Austrian position in the presence of first joint results. We are also guilty of this ourselves, the Parliament, the country, to report on what has been debated at the Intergovernmental Conference. This is actually the counterposition, the antithesis to the ever-increasing accusation of veiling. This is not an suppression of discussion, but it is already the clear target and the announcement that we will then put ourselves in this public debate and debate in the House. Ladies and gentlemen! The question of neutrality is, of course, always linked to the question of solidarity. And Austria has always understood well how to combine these two objectives. I reject constructing or leaving a contrast between neutrality and solidarity. It also seems to me to be appropriate again, precisely because I do not regard neutrality and solidarity as opposites, here and today, in view of this debate, which I think is important to the ten thousand Austrians, who have exercised this solidarity as members of a neutral state, to thank the ten thousand Austrians in the context of UN units in the various parts of the world for their use, because this is a typical and clear sign, a symbol of how neutrality and solidarity fit together. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] It's true, it's also been mentioned: Austria has not been afraid to participate in WEU peacekeeping actions under the Common Foreign and Security Policy, such as the posting of police officers to Mostar. We have understood this as a means of maintaining peace, a means of expressing solidarity. In this sense, we have also participated by sending a unit to IFOR to Bosnia. This shows that we are not concerned about contact, but that we are examining participation in international actions on the basis of the law in force and depending on the objective of the operation in question on a case-by-case basis and then, after examination, we decide whether to take part in them or not. But that means we're not pushing anything, we're not afraid of anything. However, we are always accountable for what our security serves, and that is why, I think, we have done a very good job in recent Austrian history. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] There is therefore no reason or need for a discussion on participation in a military alliance. I understand: In a free society, such discussions cannot be forbidden, and that would not fit into our free and democratic system at all. The question is only – and everyone has to decide for themselves: How useful is it to start a discussion, for example, about membership in NATO, when you know that this NATO itself is only at the beginning of a discussion and transformation process? . I believe that for Austria, therefore, neither for security policy nor for general policy reasons is there a need to discuss membership in a military alliance. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] If at some point it is concrete to deal with such a question and to re-evaluate an essential element of Austria's political self-understanding, I believe that, as you have just quoted me, a referendum should certainly be taken into account. However, since neither NATO accession nor membership of a military alliance is part of government policy, I consider that this question is not up-to-date, even if I have to repeat it over and over again. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! To your request: The invitation to the Austrian Federal Government to act in accordance with the law is therefore not necessary, as the Federal Government, within the meaning of Article 18 of the Federal Constitutional Act, naturally adheres to the current legal situation in all its activities. This includes compliance with constitutional provisions on neutrality. For the participation of Austria in the European Union's Common Foreign and Security Policy, the Constitutional Legislator created an explicit constitutional basis in 1994 in which we move. This is also true of the participation of Austria in the Intergovernmental Conference. In this context, I expressly refer to the Austrian position of principle on the Intergovernmental Conference, which went to the House. As Austria has great interest in an efficient common foreign and security policy of the Union, it is very likely that the position will be that individual non-military issues of foreign policy could be transferred to the principle of majority voting. For the military sector, Austria – and this also applies to all other Member States – is at least for maintaining the principle of unanimity. It should also be noted that, in view of the new security challenges in Europe, Austria is very interested in carrying out humanitarian tasks and rescue operations and peacekeeping tasks and crisis management, in other words the Petersberg tasks, on a broad political decision in Europe. We therefore believe that these decisions should be taken within the framework of the European Union in accordance with the Charter of the United Nations. The mandate for the implementation of these decisions by the WEU should be possible from the European Union, which may only be done on the basis of decisions taken by the United Nations. Austria's participation in such measures is possible as an EU full member and as an observer of the Western European Union, in accordance with the usual practice, and accession to a military alliance is not necessary for all these reasons. Ladies and gentlemen! From today's point of view, this newly described development possibility in the EU's relationship with the WEU is a possible, perhaps likely result of the current Intergovernmental Conference. This result would not affect the legal foundations of Austria's neutrality, but would nevertheless allow Austria to participate in solidarity on a case-by-case basis. That is why the question of joining a military alliance does not arise, and otherwise I refer to my previous remarks. Ladies and gentlemen! I invite you to take the opportunity, not least through our future representative in the European Parliament, to occupy and shape all the areas of policy that really meet people's safety needs. Let us concentrate on the problems, on the concerns that people really care about and their solution conveys a direct, individual sense of security in which we make sure that policies for employment, for social security, for a clean and healthy environment and set out the rules to counter the dangers of a comprehensive globalisation. I am convinced, ladies and gentlemen, that in this way, in order to put the options clearly on the table and not to allow us to move forward from very quickly generated demands to join a military alliance, we do not allow the real and real security problems of our homeland to distract us. We are working together to ensure that we not only discuss academically here, but that we give the people a clear picture by means of clear and concrete actions as regards internal security, cooperation with other countries in the field of security policy, then we will make a good contribution to security in Austria.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e13041	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen of the Liberal Forum, in Question 6/A, put a question on the subject of the repeal of the opening act. The subject of opening hours has repeatedly been the subject of discussions and discussions in this House, and I would therefore not like to concentrate on the various details and history of the development of this law, but rather to make some fundamental comments on this your request and also on its reasons. It is mentioned in the explanatory statement, among other things – I may quote – that: A comprehensive new regulation of working time and the repeal of the opening law will not lead to longer working hours, weeks and annual hours. My very much appreciated Ladies and gentlemen! However, all our previous experience in close contact with trade workers shows that this is different in practice, namely that personal working hours, the situation and the duration of working hours are adapted to longer hours, that capacity-oriented working hours are used more and more part-time workers but also minor employees. This type of work is used for longer hours. This means that an extension of the opening time de facto has a concrete impact on the working situation, on the working time situation of the workers, and therefore also very personal effects, which, in particular, result in additional burdens for the many colleagues employed in this sector – we have over 70 percent of women employed there. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! It is very often stressed here that workers' protection was ensured by increased in-house co-determination. I see – and now I say: unfortunately – such a development in no way. Especially in trade, workers' protection provisions are not taken very seriously. There are several major complaints and also reports from the labour inspectorate that there are massive violations of worker protection provisions in this sector. I therefore also look forward to the support articulated here by the applicants on the issue of increased co-determination, and I am curious to see how far it will be possible to improve negotiations with the collective contractual partners, but not least in the law, the co-determination rights of the individual colleagues in the firms, but also their interests and works councils in such a way that more time autonomy, more sovereignty and more personal law can be implemented. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen from the Liberal Forum! You also write in your application: The complete abolition of all closing times is also one of the essential conditions for strengthening Austria's economic location as well as the flexibility of working time and so on. I do not think that Austria's economic location will be improved by a total abolition of the opening law. I believe that there are important factors for the attractiveness of the economic location, such as a functioning infrastructure, qualified and motivated employees, orderly employers' and employees' relations, a good cooperation between localised companies and the government and also the social partners, the form of how we live in our country, but also the fact that we have stable social conditions. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In the word "stable social conditions" it is very important to point out that this also means considering each other. The different sectors of interest are to be viewed and respected with tolerance, and one has to take account of the other and see where there are needs. Mr Peter has spoken here today about confidence in freedom of action, and he is saying that there should be no legal shackles. I think that a society, as we imagine it and as we want it, must also have confidence in solidarity, must also have confidence that the stronger are on the side of the weaker and that the weaker do not come under the wheels. [[Abg. Dr. Schmidt: Yes!]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I believe that we are called upon to ensure that individualism, which has become stronger in our society over the last forty and fifty years, does not end in an unrestricted egoism, so that individualism is no contradiction to solidarity. [[Abg. Dr. Schmidt: That's it!]] Dear ladies and gentlemen! I would also like to say something about the concern of employees in this sector. Once again, reference was made to working time: All our surveys show that an extension of the opening hours also concerns individual working hours. Two thirds of the retailers who pay overtime do not receive overtime supplements and no corresponding fee. More and more, part-time periods are carried out but not paid to the extent provided for in collective agreements. A special characteristic of this industry: Final work and preparatory work before and after the opening hours are not included in normal working hours. I therefore believe that it is very important that the collective agreement improves the framework conditions in such a way that the demands of employees can be implemented and taken into account. Let me also say a few words about the public debate on the opening hours. It gives the impression that the population wants to have a limitless opening time to shop around the clock. We have made substantial investigations and recently made a telephone survey among consumers and traders and interested parties on the opening hours, and I would like to quote a few comments from this telephone survey: 86 percent of consumers are able to do their purchases easily with the opening hours currently offered. It is also noted that 85 percent of dealers are not considered to be important to extend daily opening hours by 9 p.m., but 84 percent of consumers are of this opinion. Similarly, it is during the opening of the shops on every Saturday afternoon. Here 82 percent of dealers say: not important, and 71 percent of consumers: not important! [[Abg. Dr. Schmidt: You should only give them the opportunity!]] Dear Mrs. Dr. Schmidt! There are already many possibilities to offer extended opening hours under the legal provisions of the opening law, but these are not used by consumers in the form as always tried to give the impression. Nevertheless, ladies and gentlemen, I am very glad that there are concrete but very constructive negotiations and discussions between workers' and employers' interests on this issue, and there seems to be a consensus on extending the opening hours with appropriate framework conditions. From my point of view, however, one thing is in no way acceptable: that on the one hand one is expected to lock up even longer, more often locked up, but goes on the other side and takes away supplements. We give this intention a clear rejection! That can't be a concept! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Social partnership arrangements should therefore be made to improve the quality of shopping for consumers on the one hand, but also to take into account the quality of working time for workers on the other. I am back where I believe that there must also be better co-determination and that appropriate legal and collective framework conditions must also be created. These are now under negotiation. I hope that a positive conclusion will be reached. My very much appreciated Ladies and gentlemen from the Liberal Forum! However, I would like to talk about one point where I would like to give you very much in principle. They write that the strengthening of Austria's economic location needs a whole set of measures. I have mentioned some of these measures. I would like to point out that I believe that training opportunities and further improvements in our training are part of this. I do not believe that the repeal of the opening law is part of that. I think it is not an appropriate means of gaining even more attractiveness as an economic location in Austria. I believe that a limitless liberalism endangers social cohesion, but not only on this issue, but in principle. I'll be back at the beginning. I think that a society, as we want it to be, must be excellent in reaching out to one another, taking account of one another, exercising tolerance and assisting the weak in our society. Overcoming conflicts of interest, without overestimating, and helping the weaker: That's our policy! – but not an unlimited liberalisation, which ultimately only harms everyone.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e13243	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen of the Liberal Group! We very often have great agreement in the area of fundamental rights and also in many social policy areas. There, Greens and Liberals often have similar ideas and draw on a common strand. But when it comes to economic policy, and in particular your measures of liberalisation, unilateral liberalisation, as I believe, at the expense of good social security, really separate worlds. [[Abg. May. Firlinger: Where, Mrs. Colleague?]] Above all, I think that the proposals relating to the principle of the abolition of a shop opening system in Austria are wrong. I think that this radical economic liberalism, which you represent here, is one of the oldest errors, and it has shown itself a hundred years ago in which direction it leads. It is therefore also incomprehensible to me, which is why you, both the government and the Greens, are constantly blaming that the government and also we old-fashioned concepts represent, although it is actually true that this kind of acceleration of pure capitalism was the most original and oldest concept, which has simply resulted in the fact that not only at that time there was no social protection, but above all in no further development of democracy in Europe. Democratic standards and social standards must not be separated. It is all the more surprising to me that this contradiction exists with you and that you are not trying to see and discuss with us the real damage that would come about through such a regulation, and the loads that would happen to women in this area. One of your arguments in the debate on opening hours is that there would be more jobs due to flexibility in the field of labour market policy. However, the concrete examples in other countries show that this is a mistake. Both in the United Kingdom and in the United States, it has been shown that more jobs have not been created, but - and I am giving Mrs Hostasch a 100% right - that unemployment has hidden behind the notions of temporary and minor and part-time jobs the problem has really been seen far too late and that we are getting rid of it from a goal we hope to have all, namely full-time employment. If there is a consensus on this and if our political objective is to ensure full employment in Austria and in Europe, then a general abolition of shop opening times or the notions of radical liberalisation and deregulation as you wish can not be consistent with it. Although I also know and share your personal experiences, we who sit here are extremely privileged due to our jobs. Each of us has the experience of standing in front of blocked shops at 6 p.m., and at the weekend, especially if you had time to buy us as a stressed politician or manager – or whatever – the opportunity to buy. We are extremely privileged in this way and have a completely different perception. But if I talk very consciously to the cashier women from the various supermarkets in Vienna, I simply experience and hear that it is not the exception, but will become more and more the rule that women who get up early at 4 o'clock are slightly employed in Vienna, part-time jobs, no longer dare to make such demands. That's no longer a phantom, it's not a danger, it's actually a reality in trade! I am right to say that we have to deal more imaginatively with the division of our working time and with the phenomenon of unemployment and to develop new models, that there is much more to demand, above all, from the Social Democrats and also from trade unions and workers' representatives, that we have had the problem for many years, so I think, tossed and misunderstanded. There is certainly the need to adapt to different new developments or also to the regulation, as it will now come into force in Germany. But it is important not to lose sight of the overall objective of full employment. In particular, fundamental rights, social rights, for which many people have struggled for many years, are not simply being dealt with as quickly as possible. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] It was very much in Austria this week to read and hear about globalisation, the consequences of globalisation and the acceleration of capitalism, as we have known it in recent years. A book has been presented, many articles have been published. I believe that this Parliament must adopt itself far more than before and much faster than before. I believe that we must develop far more than before concepts which really stand up to these changes, but on the other hand we must not be ashamed to preserve existing rights, democratic rights, social rights. It is not a question of being able to compete with East Asian or with American markets. It cannot be our model to achieve social standards similar to those in East Asia or America. Our aim must, however, be to remain competitive on the one hand, but on the other hand to preserve democratic and social rights, for which one has fought for a century, and to try to preserve our democracy and democratic values at the same time. ==References== September 1996, was an article in the Financial Times, a forecast of the White House, in which one can read that in the next few years the wages in America will still be reduced by 20 percent, not only the wages of industrial workers, but above all the wages in the service sector. The reason for this is simple: Due to the new markets, the new competition, there is simply an incredible pressure on existing workers' relations and a real "dividing apart" in our society. If we give in to this pressure one-sidedly – I have often said in this House that it is time to face this problem far more offensively – then we will experience similar developments in Austria and, above all, we will have to live with the phenomenon of rising unemployment and less and less and less full employment. In short, the trade regulations and their ideas are: I have already mentioned in this context that we are also in favour of a concentration of procedures, that we also believe that some sort of debureaucratisation is possible, but it is important for us to always take environmental costs into account when granting facilities, and that we must not fall behind the achievements so far. It is necessary to discuss these issues in a serious way. It is important that you submit these applications. I very much hope that the committees will be able to discuss the consequences of globalisation, the consequences of this competition policy, and that we will be able to find very quickly answers that are practical for Austria, but also with regard to European standards. - Thank you.

Some comments as a consumer, but also as an employee representative on the Liberals' request for opening-up law. I think that statistics can sometimes be manipulated or interpreted differently, especially since a 1994 market survey has already shown that 55 percent of the workers surveyed said they want to buy longer in the evening, and also on Saturday, as far as possible, all day long. So it can't be quite true when it says that 86 percent are satisfied here. I am also concerned with interpretation, and I am very much concerned with trade workers, especially since most of them are women. But it is also about how we are now dealing with the opening hours in Austria or in this Europe. It also shows the other countries, such as Germany, that it is possible to do something more consumer-friendly with a liberalisation of shop opening times – not with a general withdrawal. I also believe that we need to think and do not pursue a policy of fear in relation to these women workers when we are talking about shop opening times, for example, by saying that this would be a total deterioration for these trade workers. It is true that the liberalisation of shop opening times does not mean that we must work 50 or 60 hours instead of 38.5 hours, but it must be so that we can do the work all the time. There is also a calculation which states that 8 000 jobs could be created in addition. I mean, especially now that it is necessary for us to create more jobs, and also for women, as women's unemployment is increasing more and more, this is certainly an argument that we also need to consider the issues clearly and carefully: How do we deal with the flexibility of working time? How do we deal with the Labour Peace Act? What can we do to get more employees – especially women – and not less? I think that if one speaks of the fact that this would be an extension of working time, that is not the right thing to do. There must be only a clear system, including the way in which the surcharges look. If, for example, one meets in the middle of the supplements, this would be a possible approach for both employers and employees in the course of this opening-up debate. I think it is a good thing to talk about KAPOVAZ, but to say that everything is drifting towards a small number of employees and work on call, I do not think it is right. Members who do not have Austrian citizenship can also be nominated in this Committee. This too is an important intermediate step and an important institution. [[Abg. Oillinger: What about the Austrians?]] I am also saying this because this is not so obvious, because we know these problems from some of the associations that have been consulting foreigners. In Lower Austria, for example, this has become quite obvious and clear. In Austria, we have a total of 270 000 foreign workers, at least 160 000 from ex-Yugoslavia. If there is a passive right to vote for foreign workers, it is also important to ask how long a person in Austria has to be employed in order to obtain this passive right of vote. Two years have been mentioned in your application, i.e. the period during which you are still entitled to work permits. It would also be possible to limit this period by five years, i.e. with the receipt of the licence. There is already a significant argument that, after two years, the foreign worker is, of course, in a particular, beyond the normal level of dependence on the worker, which then normalizes itself with the license, so that it is already necessary to discuss what period should then be chosen. I believe that this should be discussed in detail in the committee responsible. There's plenty of time left. The next election of the workers' chamber is in 1999, and – it is my concern to add – there will be a number of other wishes by the workers' chambers, in particular concerning the right to vote and the election to the Chamber of Labour, because it is quite simply clear that the current electoral order of the workers' chambers is actually passing through the needs of the workers, because it is no longer with the mobility on the labour market. For example, during the last election of the Chamber of Workers, we have had the need by the law to instigate people, where the job is located, in other words at the place of work, and we have found that there have been nearly 60 000 people in Lower Austria alone between the day of the year and the day of election, who have changed the place of work or the employer. We therefore absolutely need a right of vote, which is sufficiently flexible and which provides the opportunity to meet mobility in the labour market accordingly. In the Chambers of Labour there is a proposal on this, and I think it is a goal that the whole change to the right to vote is discussed in one, i.e. the question of the passive right of election for foreigners, but also the question of any other points in the Chamber of Workers' Act, as far as the right to vote is concerned. Ladies and gentlemen! The right to vote is also the last major milestone in the reform of the Chamber of Workers. The reform of the Chamber of Labour took place in the actual operation. The working chambers are modernized, newly structured, they work and, as the member survey shows, are also accepted to a very, very high degree. A last milestone is necessary. It concerns the right to vote, and all the open points in this right of vote should then be discussed at once. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e14883	We're coming to the 7th. Agenda item: First reading of Amendment No 215/A by Mrs Ing. Monika Langthalerand comrades concerning feed-in law 1996. ParlaMint-AT_1996-09-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00038_d7e14913	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! From social policy to environmental policy, although I would like to make a few comments on the last words. [[Abg. Scheibner: That's not part of it anymore!]] It was a really embarrassing debate, and it is especially regrettable that you, especially for the EIAs, want to take over only those areas of the European Union that directly benefit you. But you are obviously not interested in those points that provide better conditions in the field of human rights or participation. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] But also in the field of energy policy, which we want to discuss here, there are indeed far more advanced regulations in some European countries than in Austria. In this respect, too, one does not focus on those models that are already in place and which are, in part, very successful, but also on energy policy, one tries to secure power positions, to safeguard the influence of politics, to pay homage to a really absurd federalism and to maintain a policy that does not make any sense ecologically, but is also an economic full sense of stupidity. We have introduced and today we want to debate a federal feed-in law for electricity produced from renewable energy sources. Austria has already committed itself many years ago to achieving the Toronto target of reducing CO2 emissions and originally formulated an ambitious target: minus 20 percent CO2 by 2005. – We are still far from achieving this. Because of EU membership – this is what makes the debate exciting, both in terms of this legislative proposal and in terms of energy policy as a whole – there must be a restructuring in the E-Economie. We have a tough competition from 1998 onwards when the first step of liberalisation takes place. There must therefore be a total of new models. We propose that we take into account the restructuring of the energy sector and, at the same time, a pillar which has benefited Austria from the geological and hydrogeological conditions, namely the renewable energy sector. It was a request from the Greens four years ago that led to a five-party resolution request in consensus, according to which the electricity produced from renewable energy sources and then fed into the public network is favoured. The motion for a resolution adopted at the time invited the Minister for Economic Affairs to negotiate a good tariff with the Landesgesellschafts in order to assist the producers of electricity from wind power, solar installations, biomass and also small hydroelectric power plants accordingly. We know that these technologies are not yet marketable, that they need funding, and good feed-in schemes are one of the most important instruments for this. We have already criticised at the time when this motion for a resolution was adopted, that in our view this regulation is only a tool for aid. We thought at the time that a comprehensive law would be necessary, as it has existed in Germany since 1990. The Minister for Economic Affairs has always failed to implement the motion for a resolution. Because it has several barriers installed. First point: He has set time limits, and the deadline expires at the end of this year. That means: The reason why we are proposing to discuss it now, and why this problem needs to be solved very quickly is that at the end of 1996, all the preferential import tariffs for electricity from renewable sources will expire. From this point on, there will simply be no more promotions, and there will actually be quite specific projects, but also finalized installations in front of the ruin: the wind turbine in Michelbach and many others. There are currently more than a hundred projects in Austria, which have been in front of implementation and have been waiting for months to be decided by the House in this regard. Second point: There was also a limit on performance which we originally did not have in the House in the motion for a resolution. As a result of these regulations, there has been no optimal implementation in recent years and no good promotion of these alternative installations. Unfortunately, in connection with the word "alternative" many still believe that these technologies are simply the hobby of some ecoromant nuclei and yet nothing real. The fact is that over the last four or five years, a great deal has been done in this area, especially in the wind power which is a special concern for us. Austria would be quite predestined to use this form, because especially in winter, where we still need foreign electricity and sometimes it comes to the import of electricity, we use huge amounts of fossil energy – oil, gas – and in this time we need substitution possibilities, because the hydropower does not help. Therefore, for example, a power plant like Lambach would not be a solution at all. A solution and a real alternative would be the construction of wind turbines. There are serious studies and studies that say that up to 30 percent of Austrian electricity needs can be covered by electricity from wind turbines. It is a very optimistic number, but even if you take only half, 15 percent, it would still be a huge potential. This shows how much is actually possible in this area. What is the effect of regulatory frameworks in this area? – In Germany, the wind power lobby prevailed a few years ago. In Germany there is a federal feed-in law, which on the one hand provides particularly good tariffs for electricity from renewable energies and on the other hand has integrated additional targeted funding programmes. In Germany, since the entry into force of this feed-in law in 1991, the number of wind turbines has increased from 500 original to 3 675 plants. So, within four years, Germany has had a sevenfold increase. After all, these plants now cover 0.4 percent of the electricity needed there – even if it sounds little but still does. Only one comparison number: In Austria it is 0.0068 percent, i.e. a truly negligible size, a number that is simply embarrassing, compared to what you could do in Austria. This week, the three environmental and energy spokespersons of the opposition parties and the President of Eurosolar held a press conference on this subject. Eurosolar has calculated, and also for Austria, that this area is also interesting in terms of employment policy, that one not only has the ecological advantage of getting out of fossil energy sources but also has an important work force argument. I do not want to focus on how necessary it is, in principle, to get out of fossil energy sources, to decouple a bit from the dependence on gas and oil imports, how important it would be to use domestic resources in this area. What I have just done with regard to wind power is, of course, also true of biomass. Especially in the field of agriculture, there are opportunities to get in immediately and become an important provider of electricity. We have – I hope we will be able to show this in detail in the Committee's negotiations – considered a very comprehensive approach which is currently involved in the field of reorganisation of the electricity industry, combined with a decommissioning of the currently vertically structured e-commerce. It is about enabling and promoting decentralized small suppliers. We believe that our federal feed law, as we have prepared it, would be a very important instrument to provide immediate support. Very briefly: What does our law look like in detail? – We propose that the electricity supply companies use 90 per cent of the electrical energy produced from wind and sun, which is paid by 80 per cent of the respective household tariffs from biomass, sewage and landfill gas as well as from small-power heat-coupling plants and hydropower plants. In absolute terms, this meant that there would be a surcharge of about 50 sizes per kilowatt hour at the usual feed rates. I'm just saying, This is less than the tariff and the funding programme in Germany. We have tried to make a very realistic proposal here, one that is absolutely reasonable to the electricity company. The cost burden may sound too high for many at the first moment, but if one sees how much savings are due to rationalization measures and measures relating to the structure within the e-business, it is noted that it is negligible. I am referring to the two figures: Our proposal would mean additional loads for all nine national ETUCs of 125 million schilling, or, in terms of the cost of the electricity price, about 0.25 gross per kilowatt hour; an amount that is undoubtedly acceptable. For the other number: There are several economists – one I would like to mention: Professor Schleicher; he has recently noted this on a symposia – figures on rationalization within the e-business, which are incredible. We are not talking about millions of amounts, we are talking about billions. The lowest amount said by all the economists is that there is a savings potential of 20 billion schilling, which must be imagined, the highest of 40 billion schilling. These are the sizes you should pay attention to when you talk about our proposal. The sum resulting from this proposal is easy to carry out by the national electricity supply companies, especially when, in parallel, the structural and efficiency measures so urgently needed are put in place. And these, my lords, must definitely come, because if liberalisation comes at European level, then we simply have to lower prices, because competition is very large. If you think that it would be a contradiction if, on the one hand, I believe that prices should be lowered, and on the other hand that you have to pay subsidies, I will tell you: It is not a contradiction, it is a logical component when we talk about new energy organisation. We imagine an Austrian energy association that does not remain in nine federal structures as before. However, before a change of ownership is carried out, the structure must be really superior and reorganised. I would warn against the fact that, due to economic pressure from individual national societies, first and foremost the EVN in Lower Austria, it now hastens to changes in ownership and integration, which make a truly reasonable Austrian solution impossible. It is always the liberals who are very much for Austrian and national solutions. In the field of energy policy, a first Austrian solution makes sense because of many arguments. I believe that we must and should work with European partners in this area, but if we want to use renewable energy sources if we want to prevent nuclear power from dominates supply in Europe in the future, then first we need an Austrian solution, a reasonable structure before we can talk about ownership structures that also allow foreign participation. We shall now proceed to the vote on the request for a time limit of Dr Schmidt, the Committee on Labour and Social Affairs, to report on the motion for a resolution 284/A by Dr Schmidt and others concerning a federal law on the abolition of provisions on the social security of work contracts and so-called free service contracts. In October 1996, the Commission adopted a proposal for a Council Directive on the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the approximation of the laws of the Member States relating to the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to turnover taxes. I ask those ladies and gentlemen who are in favour of this deadline proposal to give a corresponding sign. – This is the minority. The application is rejected. Inflow
